Eleven rows carrying a number, a category, a three-line sentence and an `Open` label ran to **1929px** of text under the finder; a row of a 56px thumb, a name and an arrow was 1154px and tidy but read as a settings list; a two-column catalogue of the same photographs at tile size was 1269px and got the page sent back - a photograph of a clinic cannot tell Retina from Glaucoma at 173px; and a three-across grid of lucide glyphs in tinted wells over short names put all eleven on one screen but was sent back too, for the eleven glyphs: one visual device repeated eleven times, each one decoded before the name beside it could be read, and an empty cell at the end of the last row. **A card is a coloured edge, a number, a name and a way in - no glyph.** `.sv-tile__link` is the whole card and the link: a 5px edge down the left, clipped to the card's corners, the ledger's numeral in the serif (`07` is the Myopia Clinic on every list), the `shortTitle` in Inter Tight, and `Learn more ->` in teal at the foot; the urgent card is the one tinted card (`#fbefed`) with its number, name and `Do not wait ->` in the danger colour. The grid is 520-526px for all eleven on every phone, rows level at 124px. **The last card takes whatever columns its row leaves empty and lays out sideways** (`data-wide`, computed in the component as `spare + 1`) - on eleven that is Emergency Eye Care - so the grid closes on a full row and, sideways, its sentence costs the row no height. The sentence is rendered on every tile and CSS shows it on the wide card and on every card from 560px, where a third of the shell is 165px or more.
  - **Any choice - a symptom, a part of the eye or a kind of care; one answer or three - is the same answer card** (`.sv-answers` / `.sv-answer`), full width, one under the other: the grid's tile opened up, with the same coloured edge, the same number and the same `Learn more ->` (`Do not wait ->` and the tint on the urgent card), plus the room a tile does not have for the service's 240px `thumb` (`clamp(72px, 22vw, 96px)`), its kind of care in the edge's colour, its full title and its sentence. **This replaced three count-dependent shapes and must not grow them back**: one answer was a photo card with a serif name and a navy arrow disc (`data-single`), three were bare tiles with no sentence, and two were a tile beside a sideways card - so every symptom answered with one object and every kind of care with another, which is exactly what the hospital sent back as confusing. The list is keyed on the choice (`mode` + criterion), so a new answer is dealt fresh - the cards rise 70ms apart - rather than morphed out of the old one; nothing exits, so the page never collapses between answers. Clear, a second tap on the chosen control, or a route switch brings the grid back, fading in whole. One card is 139-144px tall, three are 477-565px.
  - **The edge's colour is the kind of care, from `data-tone` (the service's `category`), never from `nth-child`**: navy for surgical, teal for the specialty clinics and everyday care, the accent for the three children's services, the danger colour for urgent. **The care-type picker and the answer cards carry the same colours on a phone** (`data-tone` on `.sv-kind`, a 4px `::before` in the phone block; the answer card's edge and kind label), so the colour is a legend the reader can read back rather than decoration. `--sv-tone` and `--sv-tint` are set once per tone on `.sv-tile__link`, `.sv-answer__link` and `.sv-kind`.
  - **The name's size follows the viewport** (`clamp(0.78rem, 6.24vw - 8.2px, 1rem)`), because the card does: a third of a phone less the gaps and the padding is `w / 3 - 41px` of text, and `examination` - the widest single word - is 85.5px at 1rem. Stepped sizes left gaps where a single word overflowed (`examination` at 1rem in the 84px box a 381px phone gives it); the formula keeps 2px of slack from 341px up and the padding closes a step below 341px. `Learn more ->` is 80px and a card's text column only reaches that at 364px, so below 371px the label is the short form (`cardLabel` / `cardLabelShort` in `page.index`, the cookie sheet's device) - it shipped once at 320px as `earn more`, clipped at the card's edge, and the probe now checks the label's rect against the card's padding box at every width.
  - **The motion is the section's, not the card's.** The tiles arrive on a 40ms stagger the first time the grid is on screen and each edge draws down 200ms behind its card (`data-in` on the list, `--i` on the card; the entrance lives on `.sv-tile__link`). **The observer is a callback ref (`watchGrid`), not an effect and not framer's `useInView`**: the grid unmounts while an answer is shown, and a reader who chooses before scrolling down has to get the entrance on the grid that comes back, not on a node an effect saw once - verified by choosing with the grid 650px below the fold, clearing, and scrolling to eleven tiles at opacity 1. A press scales a tile to 0.97 (an answer card to 0.98), tints it with its tone and widens the edge to 8px; a fine pointer's hover lifts it and leans the arrow. Under `prefers-reduced-motion` both are simply there (`gridShown` short-circuits, the answers take no variants, and the CSS sets every card and edge to its finished state) - 0 running animations after a choice.
  - Contrast, computed on the solid grounds: numeral 7.8:1, name 18.2:1, sentence 7.8:1, `Learn more` 6.2:1 on white; the answer card's kind label 14.3:1 navy, 6.2:1 teal and 6.4:1 accent; the urgent number, name, label and kind 6.5:1 and its sentence 6.9:1 on the tint. The card's hairline is `--e-rule` with a soft shadow: the edge, the number and the teal label identify the control, not the border.
  - Between 641 and 980px the rows stay rows, with the sentence under the title (`grid-template-areas`) and the picture and the action spanning both lines - 1455px at 768px. Measured with `Range.getClientRects()`: no card name clips or exceeds two lines at 320, 340, 341, 350, 360, 370, 371, 375, 380, 381, 390, 412, 430, 560, 600 or 640px, no row title wraps at 768-1180px, and none wraps at 1280-2560px with the desktop columns at `5.6fr / 4.4fr`.
- **The opening on a phone is a masthead**: below 721px the eyebrow and the iris share the first row - `Services` at the left, the mark (`min(30vw, 120px)`) at the right - and the headline runs under both. `.sv-top__copy` takes `display: contents` so its parts hold the grid's rows (it is a plain div). The mark used to stand alone above the eyebrow, a small dot at the left with two thirds of its row empty, and cost 70px of the first screen: the whole opening, both actions included, now fits a 390x664 (the `Emergency eye care` link was under the fold), and the stamp's two figures wrap as a pair (`.sv-top__figures`), so a 320px phone puts both under the label rather than leaving `6 hospitals` alone on a line. The opening and the explorer are one ground, so the explorer's top padding closes up (`.sv-top + .sv-explorer`): their two paddings met as 96px of empty paper on a phone before the explorer's rule.
- **The head's lede is hidden below 641px** (the contact and appointment heads' device): the tabs and the readout's hint say the same thing, and on a phone it stood between the heading and the instrument. **On a phone the status line is the results' own header** (`.sv-sum`, rendered in place of `.sv-status` when `phone`): a serif title - `What we treat` with nothing chosen, `Suggested for you` after a choice - with `Show all 11` beside it (`Show all` below 371px, `showAllShort`, so the title keeps one line on the SE), and a line under both reading `Because you chose <criterion>` with `2 of 11 services` on its own line (on one line the count broke `Specialty / clinics` in half). It replaced a count, an outlined `Clear` and a teal criterion chip, three control styles that read as three things rather than one answer. The line is the live region and stays mounted; the span inside it is keyed so a new reason fades in. `resultsRef` moves to it, so `revealAnswers` still caps its scroll at this header.
- The pane follows **scroll position as the base signal** with a pointer overriding it, so it is never stale on a touch screen.
- **The pane is hidden below 1180px, not 1024px, and that threshold is measured rather than chosen.** At 1024px it appeared the moment there was room for a box, not the moment the layout could afford one: between 1025 and 1100px the list was squeezed to 622-669px, the description column fell to **160-177px**, and two row titles wrapped. The list keeps the full width until there is room for both, and the description column now never drops below 227px at any width.
- **`.e-shell` caps at 1240px but a `vw`-scaled title does not.** Row titles first wrapped _more_ at 1920px than at 1280px because the column had stopped growing and the type had not. Measured with `Range.getClientRects()`: no row title wraps at 1280, 1440, 1920 or 2560px.
- Row titles carry `text-wrap: balance` for the widths where they have to wrap at all - without it "Comprehensive Glaucoma Care" left "Care" alone on a line at 320px.

### SymptomScene

`src/components/SymptomScene.jsx` is the symptoms route's counterpart to the anatomy drawing. There a patient points at _where_ the trouble is; here they recognise _what it looks like_. An eye chart is the one image every patient already associates with eye care, and every symptom degrades it in a recognisable way.

**It is the Snellen chart, redrawn in SVG rather than embedded as an image** - the eight standard rows from the lone E down to `L T I P H`, with the acuity fractions `20/200` to `20/20` in a column on the right, portrait like the one on an examination-room wall. A raster was rejected for three reasons: it would blur under its own effects (the effects _are_ blur), it could not be themed, and it would ship bytes for a thing eight lines of text describe exactly. The letters are the site's serif at 700 with a hairline stroke of the same ink (`paint-order: stroke fill`), which squares the serifs off toward the block optotypes of a printed chart without loading a slab face.

- **One chart per route, never one per option.** For a while every symptom row carried its own 60px thumbnail of the chart beside a label and a ring, on the argument that a reader could compare all thirteen at a glance. Measured against the reader instead: at 60px blur, haze, glare and clear all read as the same fuzzy E, so the thumbnails compared nothing, and fourteen charts, thirteen rings and thirteen 72px boxes were what made the route look like a control panel on both a laptop and a phone. The `compact` render path, `E_VIEW` and the `.sv-scene--compact` rules went with them. The large chart is the one preview: it mirrors the hovered or chosen chip, beside the chips on a laptop and pinned at the top of a phone.
- **Effect strengths are variables** (`--sv-blur` and friends on `.sv-scene`), so a smaller reading of the chart - the service detail page's readout - can soften them without touching the component.
- **The options are the console's own `.sv-chip` pills** (`sv-chip--symptom`), the control the eye route's part chips already are, so the two visual routes share one vocabulary: a white pill with the `--sv-edge` boundary, a navy fill that grows a check at its left edge when chosen, a lift on hover and on keyboard preview (`data-preview`). They wrap under their group heading beside the chart from 901px - two or three to a line at 1440px, 499px for all thirteen against a 603px readout, and no label wraps inside a chip from 960px to 2560px - and are full-width rows below (see the explorer). Two earlier shapes were both worse: bare rows with a ring at the far edge left the mark 400px from its label, and two columns of boxed rows at 1180px was a grid of twenty-six objects to read before choosing one.
- **The chart is sized like a chart on a wall, not a poster, and it sits on the well.** At half the console a portrait chart was 1050px tall and ran past the fold; the column is capped at 340px, which fits a laptop screen with the sticky offset and shows all eight rows. `.sv-notice > .sv-scene` carries the same tinted, inset-edged frame `.sv-parts__frame` gives the eye - the chart, its `How it can look` eyebrow, the name and the hint inside one object - so the readout reads as the instrument's display rather than a picture floating beside a list. **The caption carries `min-height: 102px`**, room for the idle eyebrow, `Clear vision` and a two-line hint or for a two-line name, and the hint leaves the layout (`display: none`, not opacity) the moment a symptom is shown; both are what keep the well at exactly 603px in every state, measured, so nothing resizes as the pointer crosses the chips.

- **Hover or tap a chip and the chart shows it.** `visual` on each concern names the effect - `blur`, `haze`, `glare`, `floaters`, `spots`, `tunnel`, `dark`, `double`, `droop`, `clear` - and CSS draws it as a filter on the chart or an overlay above it, so a new concern needs one word in the JSON and nothing in the component. One 480ms transition on the chart is what makes moving between symptoms a morph rather than a swap.
- **The effects are how patients describe their sight, not clinical findings.** Tunnel vision for a family history of glaucoma and floaters for flashes are the standard patient-education images; nothing in this frame may ever read as a diagnosis, and the console's note says so directly beneath it.
- **Preview and choice are separate state here too** (`previewConcern` / `concern`), exactly as for the eye parts. Hover and keyboard focus change the chart; only a click narrows the list. Verified: hovering all thirteen chips shows ten distinct visuals and leaves the list at eleven rows, hovering while a chip is chosen previews without un-narrowing, and moving the pointer off the block returns the chart to clear. **Test that reset with a real pointer** (`Input.dispatchMouseEvent`) - React's `onMouseLeave` is synthesised from `mouseout`, so a raw dispatched `mouseleave` event does nothing and reads as a bug.
- The chart is `aria-hidden` and the chips carry the words. Under `prefers-reduced-motion` the floaters hold still and every effect renders in its finished state.
- **Below 901px the chart is a landscape readout above the symptom pager, not a fixture above a list and not dropped.** It is the same well, laid out landscape and full width: a cut of the chart's top rows on the left (`preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMin slice"`) and the words on the right, with the `How it can look` eyebrow above them from 361px up. **While nothing is shown, the hint takes the name's line** (`[data-idle]` hides the label there): a phone has no hover, and `Tap a description to see how people often describe it` is worth more than `Clear vision` under letters that are plainly clear. The moment a chip is tapped its name has the line, in accent. Two earlier phone shapes both failed: a full chart above the rows had scrolled away by the tenth row, and a 250px box centred in a full-width bar read as a stranded thumbnail previewing a symptom the thumb was nowhere near. It was then pinned under the header (`position: sticky`, with `body:has(.hd--hidden)` following the header up) for as long as thirteen rows scrolled beneath it; the pager sits directly under it now and nothing is tall enough to scroll away from, so it is `position: static` there and the two pinning rules are gone.
- **The readout's chart width follows the viewport** (`--sv-readout-w: clamp(104px, 32vw, 150px)`, height `clamp(100px, 25.5vw, 118px)`) rather than stepping at a breakpoint. A fixed 150px chart with a 104px override below 360px gave the name 142px at 390px - _less_ than the 162px it had at 360px, where the chart had already stepped down. Fluid, the name column grows with the screen: 120px at 320, 149 at 360, 169 at 390, 186 at 414. The longest label (`Flashes, floaters or a shadow in my sight`) takes 4/3/2/2 lines at those widths, the four-line hint fits the same column at 320px, and neither outgrows the chart frame: the readout is 122px tall idle and chosen on every phone from 320 to 390px, 127-132px at 412-430px and 140px on a tablet, measured, so the well never changes height under a tap and the rail under it never moves.
- Contrast, pixel-sampled worst cell: on the well tint the readout's eyebrow, hint and idle label 6.8:1 at 1440px (eyebrow 7.8:1 at 390px), the name in accent 5.5:1 at 1440px and 5.7:1 at 390px; group labels 7.8:1 on the paper; the chart letters 17.6:1; symptom chips 17.7:1 idle and 13.7:1 chosen, their `--sv-edge` boundary 3.48:1. In the list: the run heading and its count 7.0:1 on the paper tint, the phone number badge 5.9:1 in accent on its white pill (navy when idle, higher). **Sample a padded pill from the text's own `Range` rect, not the element's** - the element rect puts a cell 2px inside the 4px rounded corner, where the photograph shows through, and reports 2.5:1 for a glyph that is never there. The readout's hairline is `--e-rule` like every other band rule - it is not a control, so 1.4.11 does not apply to it. Kind cards: label 18.2:1 idle / 14.3:1 selected, members 7.8:1 / 10.9:1, numbers 7.8:1 / 7.6:1, count 7.1:1 / 8.7:1. Readout figure 16.2:1, criterion chip 5.1:1.

### EyeModel - the real eye

`src/components/EyeModel.jsx` is the second route's answer to "where is the trouble": **a real human eye the reader can turn**, which turns by itself to show whichever part is asked about and drops a pin on it. A patient knows _where_ the trouble is long before they know what it is called - "the back of my eye" is a thing anyone can point at, "retina" is not - and a model of the eye they can hold says that better than any diagram. It also carries the Overview tab of every service page (non-interactive there). It replaced `EyeDiagram` as the picture; `EyeDiagram` stays as its fallback.

**It is WebGL with no library, and that is what reverses the old decision against it.** The drawing's section used to record a WebGL model as considered and rejected - 150KB+ of three.js, a GPU cost on patients' phones, a canvas with no accessible structure. None of the three holds for this build:

- **No library.** The eye is spheres, ellipsoids, slabs, cylinders and the spaces between them, so `src/lib/eyeScene.frag` solves every ray _exactly_ (analytic intervals, CSG by interval subtraction) instead of marching it. Only the four recti - bands whose thickness changes along their length - are sphere-traced, and only inside the 0.065-thick shell they live in. `src/lib/eyeRenderer.js` is the WebGL1 setup, the device timing, the camera, the springs and the picking. Shipped: the shader is 46KB of source and 37KB on the wire (see `glslSource()` below), 10.7KB gzipped. The eye rides in the services pages' shared chunk (`SymptomScene-*.js`, 61.7KB / 20.0KB gzipped - 1.3KB more than before the deployment pass below) and nowhere else.
- **GPU cost - it stops by itself.** It draws only when something moves: 60fps while the eye turns, and a few seconds of life after anything happens to it - the slow sway while nothing is chosen, the breathing glow on a chosen part, the light running along the rays, the tear in the duct - held to 30fps and then eased to rest (`LIVE_MS`, 6s, and `vitality`, which scales every one of them to 0 over about 3s; the shader's running light and tear read it as `uLive`). **A reader reading beside the eye costs the GPU nothing.** The build before this ran the 30fps ambient for as long as the eye was on screen - chosen or not, and all the time a service page's Overview was being read (measured 150 draws every 5s in all three states). Now: the route opening, a part chosen, the eye scrolled back into view, a drag let go - each starts the window again; measured 30fps for ~6s, then 0 draws, about 9-10s after the last thing that happened, on /services and on a service page, phone and desktop. Nothing draws off screen or in a hidden tab (`IntersectionObserver`, `visibilitychange`), and under reduced motion only a pose change or a drag draws.
- **Sized to the device it lands on.** The first time an eye is shown in a visit, `calibrate()` times the heaviest picture the model makes (`PROBE`: the fundus close up through the open quarter with the rays on): a 96x72 frame first - enough to tell a GPU too slow to bother with, without asking it for a full frame - then real frames at the scale that suggests. It draws at the sharpest of `SCALES` (2, 1.75, 1.5, 1.25, 1 device pixels per CSS pixel; a 1x monitor's ideal is 1.5, which is the model's anti-aliasing) whose heaviest frame fits `FRAME_BUDGET_MS` (12ms), at 1 if none does but 1 still costs under `FALLBACK_MS` (40ms), and **falls back to the drawing** if not even that. The soft shadows go with the first step down. The cost per pixel is kept for the visit (`costPerPixel`), so the next eye does not time anything, a resize only re-picks the scale, and the slow sway is drawn less often than 30fps on a GPU whose frame is dear (`ambientEvery`, never more than ~40% busy). `adapt()` is the safety net for a GPU that times faster than it runs: gaps over 34ms while the eye is turning step the scale down, and a device still crawling (gaps over 60ms) at 1x gets the drawing. Its first frame after a wake is not counted - the old version skipped every gap over 250ms, which meant the slower the device, the less it adapted. Simulated slow GPUs (a per-pixel busy-wait on every draw, which the timing and the frame gaps both see): this Mac's GPU 2x, ~5x slower 1.5x, a budget Android (~25x) 1x, ~60x and a laptop iGPU on the 652x489 desktop canvas the drawing; every case that keeps the model turns at a 17ms median frame.
- **Timed by difference, because of Safari.** Waiting for the GPU means a `readPixels`, and in Safari that is a round trip to its GPU process that costs 4-5ms - more than a whole frame of this shader on an Apple GPU (measured in Safari 27: one frame plus the round trip 4-5ms, four frames plus the round trip 4ms). The first build divided the round trip into the frame and would have drawn every iPhone below its sharpest scale. `probe()` times one frame and then three, and the difference is the frames alone.
- **Never freezes the page to compile.** The context is asked for with `failIfMajorPerformanceCaveat`, so a browser that would draw this in software says so and the reader gets the drawing; a software renderer that gets past that (one Chrome was told to use) is known by its name (`WEBGL_debug_renderer_info`: SwiftShader, llvmpipe) and refused before the shader is compiled - forced onto SwiftShader, compiling alone froze the page for 10.9s and a frame took 5.4s, with the page scrolling at two frames a second beside it. The compile runs in the browser's GPU process: with `KHR_parallel_shader_compile` (Chrome, Safari 27) the renderer polls `COMPLETION_STATUS_KHR` each frame instead of waiting, and **nothing asks for the result until it is ready**. Measured: no long task and a 17ms worst frame from pressing the eye route's tab to the first frame (a 317ms frame on a cold compile before), 33ms at 4x CPU throttle, 50ms at 6x.
- **Compiled once a visit.** An eye that unmounts parks its canvas, context and linked program (`spare` in `eyeRenderer.js`) and the next eye to mount takes them - the eye route reopened, a service page's Overview tab revisited (six round trips: the eye back in 372-389ms, the panel's own transition, on one context), React's double mount in development (one canvas, one context). A hand on the eye route's tab (`WARM_EYE` in `ServiceExplorer`: pointer arriving, finger down, keyboard focus) calls `prewarmEyeModel()`, which starts the compile before the route has opened.
- **Accessibility.** The canvas is `aria-hidden` and holds no controls; **the part chips remain the real controls**, as the old note required. Everything the model can show, a chip can choose.

**What it is, anatomically.** A left eye, seen by someone facing the patient: +z is the visual axis, nasal is -x, so the optic disc sits nasal to the fovea and the nerve leaves nasally. Units are the globe's radius (1 = about 12mm), with proportions from the eye: the cornea protrudes with a 7.4mm radius, the anterior chamber is ~3mm deep, the lens is a 9mm x 4mm biconvex ellipsoid behind a flat iris, the wall is exaggerated to 1.5mm so its layers read. **The iris is brown** - the eye most of this hospital's patients have - with wandering stroma fibres, a collarette, crypts and a dark limbal ring, not a stock blue. The wall's cut face shows sclera, choroid, the RPE line and retina in order, becoming ciliary body in front of the ora serrata; the fundus carries the disc (with its cup), a darker macula, and the arcades of arteries and veins leaving the disc (`retinalVessels`, eleven quadratic Beziers). The four recti insert along the spiral of Tillaux (medial nearest the cornea) as white tendons thickening into red bellies, cut short behind the equator the way an anatomical model shows them. The nerve's cut end shows its fascicles and the central artery and vein (seen when the reader turns the eye; the nerve pose no longer looks at it end on).

**How it shows a part** (`POSES` and `ANCHORS` in `eyeRenderer.js`): every pose is a camera (yaw from the front toward the temporal side, pitch, distance, target) plus three model states - `cut` swings the upper temporal quarter open (a wedge about the visual axis, 0 to 90 degrees), `lids` draws the lids in, `rays` sends light through. Front window: the eye in profile from the temple, so the clear dome stands out in front of the iris with its edge lit, and the pin on the dome itself (`ANCHORS.front`, 30 degrees round from the apex toward the viewer - at a true profile the apex faces away and a pin there is hidden). It was nearly frontal, and there the dome is invisible and the pin sat on the coloured part. The lens and the retina: the quarter open, looking into the section and at the fundus. The optic nerve: from the nose side, a little in front, so the nerve leaves the back of the eye with the brown iris still in the picture - it was from behind, a ball with a tube and no way to tell front from back. Eyelids and tear ducts: frontal, the lids closing in as skin with lashes, the lacrimal gland and the drainage (puncta, canaliculi, sac, duct) drawn as an x-ray under the skin with a tear running down it. Eye alignment: from the temporal side, above. Focusing power: five rays enter through the opened quarter, bend at the cornea and the lens, and meet on the fovea. The idle pose is the classic cutaway three-quarter view with the rays at half strength. **The model opens on the patient looking at the reader** (the intro pose, pupil dilated) and turns and swings open once it is on screen and the opening curtain has lifted (`useIntroDone`).

**Highlight, context and the pin.** The chosen part gains an accent edge that breathes (`uPulse`); everything else ghosts toward a light grey (`emphasise`, 42% at most) - except what gives the part context (`contextKeep`: the eye behind the lids, the iris seen through the cornea, the disc on the retina, and the iris again for the nerve and the muscles - seen from the side, a grey front left the reader unable to tell which end was which). **The white of the eye is never lit in the accent**: a magenta ring round the iris read as an inflamed eye, the one picture an eye hospital must not show - Front window lights the cornea's edge instead. The pin (`.sv-model__pin`, a 14px accent dot with a ripple) is an HTML element placed by projecting the part's anchor every drawn frame, written straight to the DOM, and hidden when its anchor faces away or the part is not shown (the lens with the quarter closed, the lids retracted).

**The part's name rides above the pin** (`.sv-model__tag`, the `label` prop): the word and the place it names in one glance, which the chip beside the eye and the caption under it cannot do for a reader who does not know which part of the picture is "the retina". The services index passes the shown part's `label` (so a hover on a chip or on the eye names what it lights); a service page does not, because its caption already names its one part. The tag is placed in the same frame as the pin - centred over it, kept 6px inside the stage, dropped below the pin where there is no room above - measured once per name (`useLayoutEffect`) and hidden until the pin reaches the new part. Navy on white, 14.3:1.

**Input** (`EyeModel.jsx`):

- **Hover lights, a tap or click chooses, a drag turns, and nothing navigates.** A hover over the model calls `onPreviewPart` and lights the part **without turning the eye** - turning to a part the pointer rests on would move it out from under the pointer and oscillate. `hoverRef` is how the pose effect tells a canvas hover from a chip hover (which does turn the eye). A click clears it, so a chosen part is turned to. A pick still waiting for its frame is cancelled on `pointerleave`, or it lit a part after the pointer had gone.
- **Picking is the shader itself**: `pick()` draws an 11x11 grid of rays spanning 44 CSS px around the pointer into a tiny framebuffer with each pixel carrying the part id, and the part nearest the centre wins - so a pick can never disagree with the picture, and the fovea or a nerve seen end-on is still a 44px target. The ids are the surfaces' own (`solidPart`, the layers' `part`), which is also what the highlight reads.
- **A finger turns the eye sideways only** (`touch-action: pan-y`): a vertical swipe over the model scrolls the page. Verified with real `Input.dispatchTouchEvent` at 390x844: a horizontal drag turned the eye and left `scrollY` alone, a vertical drag scrolled the page 234px, taps on the rays, the lens section and the fundus chose Focusing power, The lens and The retina, and taps on empty space chose nothing. A flick carries on turning for a moment; 3.5s after the finger lifts the eye turns back to the part it is showing (`RETURN_AFTER_MS`). **Under reduced motion the reader can still turn it by hand** - direct manipulation is not animation - and it stays where they leave it until another part is chosen.
- The hint is a caption **under** the stage, never over it: every corner of the picture is filled by one pose or another, and a pill laid over the globe covered it on a 320px phone. On the services index it says the eye is a control too - `Drag to turn the eye, or click a part` / `Swipe to turn the eye, or tap a part` (`finder.modelHintChoose` / `modelHintChooseTouch`, swapped under `(hover: none)`); on a phone the chips are below the first screen, and a tap on the eye is the quickest way in. A service page's eye chooses nothing and keeps `Drag to turn the eye` / `Swipe to turn the eye` (`modelHint` / `modelHintTouch`). One line at 320px.

**Layout.** `.sv-model` is the stage (`aspect-ratio: var(--sv-model-ratio)`) plus the hint. On the services index **the eye route lays out beside its parts from 901px** - the model at 4:3 in the left column (`1.55fr`), the caption and the seven chips as a full-width legend down the right (`grid-template-areas`) - the symptoms route's chart-beside-chips shape, and it made the console _shorter_ than the old drawing's (742px against 802 at 1440). Below 901px the model is stacked above the caption and chips at 16:10, 10:9 on a phone. The service page's `.sd-area` sets 5:4.

- **The whole eye fits the screen at every size.** The stage takes a height cap from its frame (`--sv-model-max`: `max(200px, 100svh - --hd-height - 190px)` on the index, `- 150px` on a service page) and keeps its full width; the renderer fits the eye to the shorter side, so a capped eye shrinks and centres rather than being cut. It was 489px tall on a 1366x657 laptop - the most common window - with the eye's foot and the hint below the fold; now 379.
- **A phone held sideways puts the eye beside its parts** (`(max-height: 520px) and (min-width: 660px) and (orientation: landscape)`), with the parts two to a row so the column beside the eye is no taller than the eye, and a service page keeps its overview's two columns there. Stacked, the eye was 453px tall on a 390px screen and only its top showed under the tabs. Below 660px (the first SE sideways) it stays stacked and only its height is capped.
- **Choosing a part on a tablet or a sideways phone turns the eye before the page moves**: `revealResults()` waits `EYE_TURN_MS` there too, as it always did on a phone. It used to scroll on the next frame and carry the eye up the screen mid-turn.

Measured on the production build at 320x568, 360x640, 375x553, 390x664, 393x659, 430x746, 568x320, 667x375, 740x360, 844x390, 932x430, 768x1024, 820x1180, 1024x768, 1180x800, 1280x800, 1366x657, 1440x900, 1920x1080 and 2560x1440, on /services and a service page: no page overflow, no console errors, no `.sv-parts` or tab control under 44px, and the stage and its hint on one screen at every size (the 568x320 SE sideways needs the route's tabs scrolled away first). The stage is 236x212 at 320px, 346x200 beside its parts at 844x390, 652x379 at 1366x657 and 652x489 from 1440. Real touch at 390x664 and 844x390: a sideways drag turns the eye and leaves `scrollY`, a vertical drag scrolls the page ~190px, a tap on the rays chooses Focusing power with its name on the pin. All seven poses were checked at 390x664 and 1440x900 with the pin and the tag on their part.

**Traps, all found by looking at it:**

- **React mounts twice in development**, and `loseContext()` in the first cleanup left the second mount a dead context - the model fell back to the drawing on the dev server only. The renderer now owns the canvas (it puts it first in `.sv-model__stage`; React renders only the pin and the tag there) and parks it on unmount, so the second mount takes the same canvas and context. Verified on the dev server: one canvas, one context, no console errors.
- **A background tab has no screen refresh.** To check the renderer in Safari without taking over the screen, a page served by the dev server ran it in an unfocused tab with `requestAnimationFrame` replaced by a 16ms timer and posted its results to a local listener. Read the device pixel ratio from the renderer, not from `canvas.width / clientWidth` on a page without the site's CSS - a bare canvas reports its attribute size as its CSS size, and that read 1x for a renderer that had chosen 2x.
- **Solid geometry first, then the cornea.** The cornea's inner sphere reaches behind the globe's front, so from behind the eye its back surface was found _through_ the sclera, the refracted ray left the far side, and the nerve view showed a see-through disc. The solid trace decides whether the cornea is in front at all; only rays that pass it are bent and traced again.
- **Tone map colour, not coverage.** Curving a premultiplied value brightened every faint edge - the lids' fading skin - into a white halo. The frame un-premultiplies, tone maps, gamma-corrects and multiplies back.
- **The inside of a whole eye is dark.** The lamp that lights the opened eye (a fill from the viewer's shoulder, scaled by `uCut`) shone through a closed eye's pupil as red-eye; the fundus, the ciliary body and the lens behind the pupil are all scaled down with the quarter shut.
- **The room is not reflected inside the eye**: the softbox's sharp reflection on the fundus sat in the vitreous as a floating grey square.
- **Headless Chrome renders WebGL in software (SwiftShader)** unless launched with `--enable-gpu --use-angle=metal`: a close-up frame takes seconds there, and a screenshot taken too early is simply blank - three poses "rendered nothing" until the capture waited. Measure performance on the GPU, never on SwiftShader.

**`vite.config.js` carries `glslSource()`**: a shader is imported as its source text, and the plugin drops its comments and indentation (keeping newlines, which end a `#define`), so the notes that explain the anatomy stay in `eyeScene.frag` and never reach a phone - 46KB of source ships as 37KB.

**The fallback is `EyeDiagram`**, rendered with the same props by `EyeModel` when WebGL is missing, the context is refused or lost (`webglcontextlost`), the renderer is software, or the device turns out too slow (`onFail`; `isEyeModelTooSlow()` then sends every later eye in the visit straight to the drawing). Verified with `--disable-webgl` on both pages: the drawing, and no errors; forced onto SwiftShader: the drawing, with the page scrolling at a 33ms median frame beside it. Checked in Safari 27 (the user's desktop browser) as well as Chrome: `failIfMajorPerformanceCaveat` returns a context, `KHR_parallel_shader_compile` is there, the shader compiles, the first frame lands in ~50ms with the pin placed, and the device timing chooses 2x.

#### EyeDiagram - the fallback drawing

`src/components/EyeDiagram.jsx` is the cross-section `EyeModel` falls back to - drawn, not modelled: a shaded sclera lit from the upper left, a translucent vitreous, a glassy cornea with its own glint, a biconvex lens, a cylindrically shaded optic nerve and a soft contact shadow.

- **Every shaded body agrees on one light direction** (upper left). That single rule is what stops a set of gradients reading as a set of unrelated blobs; if a new part is added, shade it the same way.
- The globe's sheen, the corneal glint and the lens glint are `pointer-events: none`. They carry no information and the sheen in particular sits directly over two tap targets.
- **The drawing is `aria-hidden` and holds no controls.** The real controls are the part chips beside it - real `<button>`s with `aria-pressed`, all tab-reachable. Pointer events on the SVG are an enhancement on top of those buttons, never the only way in.
- Every region carries an **invisible `.sv-eye__hit` circle far larger than the shape it covers**: a 3px stroke is not a tap target. Measured smallest hit area is **46px at 320px** and 53-96px everywhere above it.
- `.sv-eye` centres itself with `margin-inline: auto` and grows to `min(100%, 540px)`. **The frame gives way, never the target.**
- **The chosen part comes forward and every other region dims to 0.32**, and the live region breathes a `drop-shadow` glow on the region itself. Nothing moves while a region is lit.
- The `viewBox` is cropped to `0 40 520 300`, the extent the shapes actually span.

### IrisMark

`src/components/IrisMark.jsx` is the services page's one signature visual: an iris drawn from concentric rings, breathing the way a pupil does under changing light.

- It is **SVG, not a photograph**. A stock close-up of an eye is the most generic image an eye hospital can put on a page, and a drawing sits behind type at any width without needing a scrim.
- Only the pupil moves against the rest, which is what makes the mark read as an eye adjusting rather than as a spinning graphic. Everything animates on `transform` and `opacity` only.
- It carries no information, so `prefers-reduced-motion` renders it perfectly still - `data-still` kills every animation, verified as 8 animated elements normally and 0 under reduce.
- **Tablets keep the two-column opening**; only the mark's share of it shrinks (`0.45fr` from 1024px down). Stacking at 900px put a 200px mark in a 720px row with the rest of that row empty, which read as a stranded blob rather than a masthead, and cost 200px of height at 768px. It stacks below 720px only.
- Below 721px it shares the first row with the eyebrow as a masthead (see the phone opening under ServiceExplorer): it is the one thing on the page that says "eye hospital" before a word is read, but it must never eat the fold.
- **It draws itself in**: the pupil is on screen first as the seed, the limbus draws round it (`stroke-dashoffset`, 654 = the circle's length), the body blooms and the stroma grows out of it, and the rings arrive last - on `scale` and `opacity`, which compose with the breathing and the drift (both on `transform`) rather than replacing them. `start` and `delay` come from the page, so it runs with the head's arrival and waits for the curtain on a first visit; two frames in the undrawn state come first, so the transitions run even on a page that mounts with `start` already true.
- **On a mouse or a trackpad it looks toward the pointer** (`(hover: hover) and (pointer: fine)`): `--sv-gx` / `--sv-gy` (-1 to 1) are written straight to the SVG from a passive, rAF-throttled `pointermove`, only while the mark is on screen, and back to the centre when the pointer leaves the window. The iris turns up to 16 viewBox units and the pupil 7 more (`.sv-iris__eye`, `.sv-iris__lens`, a 900ms ease), which is what reads as an eye rather than a disc sliding; the rings and the reflection stay where the light is. Nothing on touch - it needs a pointer to look at - and nothing under reduced motion. Checked in Safari 27: the draw-in, the gaze and the stamp's count behave as in Chrome.

### ServicePathway

`src/sections/ServicePathway.jsx` is the navy band: the same six steps - examination, investigations, consultation, treatment plan, treatment, follow-up - whichever service the reader picked. Saying it once here is what stops eleven service pages each repeating it.

**The section plays as a route rather than appearing as six tiles.** The rule draws itself across, a single light runs it once, then each marker lands and its step rises behind it, in order. **The order is the content** - it is a sequence, and a reader who watches it assemble left to right has already been told that. The step body is a beat behind its own marker (`0.34s + index * 0.16s`) so the dot lands and the copy rises behind it rather than the two arriving together.

- Each step lifts 3px on hover and its marker takes a soft accent ring, so the route is inspectable rather than only watchable.
- Six across at full width, three at <= 1080px, and **one column below 760px, where the route turns vertical**. The rule is dropped only in the three-column band: it can only span one row there, and drawn across row one alone it read as a route that stopped at step three.
- **The two-column phone layout it replaced was the worst of both.** No rule can join six steps across two columns, so the section stopped being a sequence at all - which is the only thing it says - and every marker was left orphaned above a block it had no visible relationship to. The vertical timeline puts the order on the axis a phone is shaped for, and costs 100px less height than the version it replaced.
- **On mobile each step carries the segment of line below its own marker** (`.sv-path__step::before`), rather than one rule spanning the list. A single rule cannot know where the last marker is - it ran 43-67px past it, down the side of the closing paragraph. Segments also draw in step with the markers instead of ahead of them, and the last step carries none, so nothing dangles. Verified: five connectors for six steps at every width below 760px.

### ServiceEmergency

`src/sections/ServiceEmergency.jsx` is the closing band and the only section on the site that is **neither paper nor navy** - a rust gradient. That is deliberate: an urgent notice that looks like every other band is a notice nobody reads.

**It is ordered act -> recognise -> meanwhile, and that order is the whole design.** The build before it opened with a five-line paragraph of chemical-exposure first aid, put two buttons of equal weight under it, and left the actual phone number as small body text at the very bottom - so a reader with a detaching retina met first aid for a different emergency before they could find the number, roughly 1500px into the section on a phone.

- **The number is the control, not a button that describes one.** `.sv-call` is a white block carrying the helpline in 2.3rem navy with a filled `Call now` inside it, and the whole block is the `tel:` link. White on the rust field is the hardest contrast this palette allows, which is what an emergency action should be. Measured: the number lands **245-301px into the section on phones**, directly under the heading.
- **The call sits beside the heading, not under it.** That row's right half was otherwise empty, and putting it there took the desktop section from 1010px to 898px without shrinking anything.
- **The nine signs run three across** (three at >= 1024px, two from 620px, one below). Nine lines in a single column is a scroll, and this is the one list a reader may be scanning in a hurry. They arrive one at a time on a stagger - a reader checks their own symptom against each line in turn, so the motion is a reveal, not decoration.
- **The chemical-exposure instruction is demoted, not removed.** It is first aid a reader may need before they reach a phone, but for one scenario out of nine, so it sits after the signs where it no longer outweighs the emergency it is part of.
- The label carries the page's **one continuously moving thing**, a slow opacity beacon on its warning glyph. It marks the section a reader must not scroll past; it is opacity only and slow enough to read as a beacon rather than a blink, and `prefers-reduced-motion` stops it dead.
- The closing route to `/services/emergency-eye-care` is a **quiet text link, not a second button**. Two buttons of equal weight made "I have an emergency" look like an alternative way to browse, which is the one thing it is not.
- Contrast, pixel-sampled on the gradient at 390, 768 and 1440px, worst local cell: label 9.6-10.2:1, heading 14.0-15.1:1, lede 10.0-10.8:1, call hint 9.3-9.7:1, signs label 8.3-8.9:1, signs 12.5-13.4:1, first-aid label 7.1-7.7:1, first-aid text 9.3-10.2:1, closing link 12.5-14.8:1. On the white call block: label 7.8:1, number 14.3:1, button 14.3:1.
- Measured with no page overflow and no target under 44px from 320px to 2560px.

### Contrast and measurement

**Measured, not assumed.** Solid-ground text was sampled with a compositing walker; the urgent band was sampled from **real painted pixels**, because its ground is a gradient and computed styles cannot see it.

- On paper: headline and row titles 16.6-18.2:1, serif accent 6.4:1, ledes 7.1-7.8:1, row text and category labels 7.1:1, filter counts 8.1:1, the opening's urgent link 7.7:1. On the navy: heading 14.3:1, step title 14.3:1, step text 8.4:1.
- On the rust gradient, worst local cell across 390, 768 and 1440px: label 8.1-10.1:1, heading 14.0-14.9:1, action label 7.5-8.2:1, action text 9.7-10.4:1, helpline 8.5-8.8:1, warning signs 12.5-14.5:1.
- **Two traps in measuring a gradient band, both of which produced false failures:**
  - `html` carries `scroll-behavior: smooth`, so a rect read straight after `scrollIntoView` is read mid-travel and the sample lands on whatever is really at those coordinates - the footer, in practice. Set `scrollBehavior = 'auto'` first.
  - Read the element's real `color` **before** setting the glyphs transparent. Reading it after returns `rgba(0,0,0,0)` and every ratio comes back as exactly 1:1.
- **Control boundaries need `--sv-edge` (`#7f8b96`), not `--e-rule-2` or `--e-rule`** - the concern chips, part chips, filter chips, the mode group and the answer cards are all controls whose border is the only thing identifying them. `--e-rule-2` measures 1.58:1 and `--e-rule` 1.18:1 against the 3:1 WCAG 1.4.11 wants; `--sv-edge` measures 3.48:1 on white and 3.16:1 on the paper tint. Same edge value the header and cookie sheet use. Do not lighten it. **The answer card caught this late** - the whole card is a `Link`, so its border is a control boundary, not decoration.
- **The explorer's ground is a gradient, so every figure on it is pixel-sampled, worst local cell** - a computed-style walker sees `background-color: transparent` and silently measures against the page behind it. Section eyebrow 7.0:1, heading 16.4:1, lede 7.0:1; tab idle 7.1:1 and selected 14.3:1; chip idle 6.4:1; the console note 9.8:1 on the clinical strip; status figure 16.2:1, the teal criterion 5.6:1, Clear 11.3:1; row number 6.4:1, title 18.2:1, category 7.8:1, text 7.8:1; the availability note 7.0:1; pane category 7.8:1, title 18.2:1, bullet 7.8:1. All eighteen pass.
- **A reveal measured off-screen reads as invisible content, and that is not a bug.** Jumping straight to the bottom of the page leaves the pathway steps and the warning signs at opacity 0 because they were never in view; scrolling to them, or deep-linking `#emergency`, shows them at full opacity. Scroll each section into view before measuring it. The `animate` prop is nonetheless always a defined object rather than `undefined`, so a trigger that never fired could not strand the content.
- **Two headless-Chrome artifacts will look like failures and are not.** `.focus()` does not dispatch focus events when the document itself is unfocused, so the part chips appear not to respond to keyboard until `Emulation.setFocusEmulationEnabled` is on - with it, focus lights the region and sets `aria-pressed`. And `global.css`'s blanket reduced-motion rule resolves to `1e-06s`, not `0s`, so a test asserting `=== '0s'` reports every transition as still live.
- Measured with **no page overflow and no target under 44px** (not merely the 24px minimum) at 320, 360, 390, 414, 768, 820, 1024, 1280, 1440, 1920 and 2560px, in both motion modes, with every route re-checked for console errors afterwards.
- Re-measured for the phone pass (the rails, the card grid and the opening) at 320x568, 360x640, 375x667, 390x844, 412x915, 430x932, 768x1024, 844x390, 1024x768 and 1440x900: no overflow, no `.sv-explorer` control under 44px, no chip label over two lines in the rail, no card name over two lines; the console 474-497px on every phone with the symptoms open, 355-394 with the kinds, 644-719 with the eye; the grid 520-526px for all eleven; the explorer about 1465px on phones against 2901 before, and the page 6295-7238px against 8012. Re-measured for the answer-card pass at 320x568, 360x640, 375x667, 390x844, 412x915, 430x932 and 600x960 with real touch taps on all 25 choices (13 symptoms, 7 parts, 5 kinds): every tap lands on its control, every answer is the same card at the same width, the first answer is fully on screen, nothing clips or overflows, and Clear brings all eleven back with the urgent card wide again; a touch tap on an answer opens its service; at 4x CPU throttle a care-type choice runs with no long task (worst frame 17ms, against 33ms for the tiles it replaced), and a symptom's 92ms task is the chart's own (118ms before this pass); a mouse press reads `matrix(0.97 ...)` with the edge at 8px (headless Chrome does not apply `:active` for an emulated touch hold). Checked in Safari as well as Chrome at phone width: the clipped edges, the numerals and the wide card render the same. Verified with real touch: a chip tap changes the chart, the count and the list and scrolls only as far as the first answer needs; a horizontal drag moves only the rail and a vertical one only the page. Under `prefers-reduced-motion` a chip tap leaves 0 running animations with the list narrowed and the chart changed. The desktop wrap, grid and ledger are unchanged (console 802px, ledger 1640px at 1440px, as before).
- **Re-measured for the deployment pass** at 320x568, 360x640, 375x553, 390x664, 393x659, 412x915, 430x746, 568x320, 667x375, 740x360, 844x390, 641x900, 768x1024, 820x1180, 900x1000, 1024x768, 1180x800, 1280x800, 1366x657, 1440x900, 1920x1080 and 2560x1440, on the dev server and the production build: no overflow at any scroll position, no target under 44px, no clipped chip, tab or card label, no tab label over one line and no chip label over two, no console errors, and 0 running animations under reduced motion. Real touch at 320x568, 360x640, 390x664, 768x1024 and 844x390: both opening actions, all four group tabs, the swipe and the vertical drag over the pager, a symptom, a part, a kind, Show all / Clear, an answer card, the wide urgent tile, the helpline and the closing link each land on their own control and do what they say. At 4x CPU throttle on the production build a real touch scroll through the page has no frame over 50ms (worst 33ms at 390x664 and 1440x900; 50ms at 6x), and the opening's arrival runs at 16.7ms a frame after the one long frame that is React committing the route. Checked in Safari 27 at 390, 768 and desktop width through a self-reporting page (the window must be visible - Safari freezes transitions and timers in a hidden page, which reads as every arrival stuck at its first frame): the arrival, the count, the gaze, the console's rule and focus pass, the pager, and the scroll-driven rises match Chrome. The service pages that share this stylesheet were re-swept (cataract, glaucoma, emergency at 390 and 1440): unchanged.

## One service: /services/:slug

**Five short blocks, and one of them is the page.** A hero, a card the reader explores, the visit, the questions, the way on - and an action bar that follows a phone reader down the screen. On a 390x844 phone the page runs 3437-3756px above the footer depending on the service: about four and a half screens.

Two builds came before this one and both were too much page:

1. The first gave thin content (two paragraphs, four bullets, two questions) the index's airy label-left / heading-centre head. Every band was two thirds empty and the page read as a well-typeset wireframe.
2. The second filled the width - a full-bleed hero, a navy includes panel, an anatomy band, a team band, a closing band - and ran to **6582px on a phone**, eight screens for one service.

**The fix was not more design, it was less page.** The detail now lives in one tabbed card instead of a stack of full-width bands, and the rule for anything new is: _if something has to go on this page, it goes in a tab._

- All copy lives in `services.json` - the page's labels under `page.detail`, and each service's own `longDescription`, `causes`, `symptoms`, `featureBullets` and `faq` under `items`. It is read through `src/lib/servicesData.js` (`serviceDetail`).
- The page is `src/pages/ServiceDetailPage.jsx`, the sections are `ServiceHero`, `ServiceExplore`, `ServiceVisit`, `ServiceFaq` and `ServiceRelated`, the two components are `ServiceActions` (the hero's row) and `ServiceActionBar` (the sticky one), and all of them own `src/styles/service-detail.css` (`.sd-`).
- It uses `.e-shell`, `.e-btn` and `.e-link` from the design system but **not `.e-head` or `.e-sec`** - both are built for bands with more copy than this page has. `.sd-head` is a flex row (heading left, one link right) and `.sd-sec` is a tighter band.
- It carries **no branch cards, no doctor rail, and does not re-explain the six steps of a visit**. The footer lists every hospital on every page, `/doctors` owns the consultants, and `ServicePathway` on the index owns the pathway.
- The page it replaced was `PageHeader` plus three `global.css` sections and the old `FAQ.jsx`. All of its rules (`.service-detail*`, `.feature-list`, and the `.e-faq*` block in `landing.css`) are gone, along with `FAQ.jsx`, whose only consumer this was.

### ServiceHero

**Built to the Function Health "What we test" reference**: an editorial head on warm paper, then one wide media card carrying the photograph and a rail of glass chips along its foot. `src/sections/ServiceHero.jsx`.

It replaced a full-bleed navy band with the copy laid _over_ the photograph. That band had to fight its own picture for contrast across eleven very different photographs and read as a stock banner. Splitting the two - **words on paper, picture in a frame** - lets the type be set properly and lets the photograph be a photograph.

Four things carry the reference and are the ones most easily lost:

1. **The headline is serif, centred, with its closing word in italic accent.** `splitServiceTitle()` takes that word off the end of the title - every service but Oculoplasty ends on the noun that names the kind of care (Surgery, Care, Services, Clinic, Examination), which is exactly the word the reference italicises. A one-word title keeps the roman face rather than being italicised whole.
2. **The card is a wide letterbox with air around it**, not a band bled to the window: `21/9` from 901px, `3/2` below it, `5/6` below 640px. Those are measured, not chosen - at `21/9` a 390px screen left 103px of visible photograph and a 320px one just 36px once the foot had taken its share. Every width now keeps 166-365px of picture.
3. **The chips are translucent glass over the photograph**, not a list under it - each one a ticked line carrying the service's own `featureBullets`. The reference writes `Included` on every card because its list runs to 160+ tests and inclusion varies; everything shown here is included by definition, so that word was the same noun printed four times under a label that had already said it. The tick carries it.
4. **Four chips share the row from 901px up; below that they are a scroll-snap rail with the next one peeking**, which is what says it can be swiped. Same mechanism as the doctor rail, and the browser's direction lock is what keeps a horizontal swipe out of the page's vertical scroll. **The rail runs to the frame's edges and fades at an end only where there is more past it** (`data-start` / `data-end` on `.sd-chips__wrap`, written from a passive scroll listener - the doctors filter's device): the second chip used to stop at the foot's padding cut mid-word (`Bladel` / `assist`) with nothing saying the rail went on. **The fade is a shade over the rail (`::before` / `::after` on the wrapper, fading into the foot's navy), never a `mask-image` on the rail**: a masked ancestor becomes the chips' backdrop root and their glass stops blurring the photograph. The wrapper exists because a pseudo-element inside the scroller would scroll away with its content.

**The chips live here and nowhere else.** `featureBullets` used to be a fourth tab in the explore card (`How we treat it`); that tab is gone, because this is the one element on the page that says what the reader actually gets and saying it twice is what the rest of this page is built to avoid. The tab set is now Overview / Why it happens / Signs to notice.

**Two contrast rules, both learned by measuring:**

- **The glass is dark, not white.** A white translucent panel _raises_ the luminance behind white type, and over these eleven photographs - a dark theatre through to a near-white clinic - the tick measured 3.7:1 and the label 2.4:1. `rgba(11, 25, 39, 0.46)` keeps the photograph visible through the chip and makes the type reliable on all of them.
- **The foot carries its own floor**, a local gradient under the label and the chips, rather than relying on the frame's scrim. A wash sized for the brightest photograph would flatten the other ten; a local floor leaves the top of every picture bright and guarantees the ground where the type actually is. Measured worst local cell across all eleven photographs at 1440px and 390px: tag 8.0:1, included label 5.2:1, chip text 15.4:1, chip tick 16.5:1.

The two glass tags at the top left - the kind of care and the part of the eye - are both derived, neither written per service. The actions sit centred under the lede on paper, so `ServiceActions` uses the filled navy button rather than the light one, and the sticky action bar still anchors to that row.

**It arrives in reading order, and on a first visit it waits for the opening curtain** (`useIntroDone()` / `isIntroPending()`, the About, doctors and services heads' rule): the back link, the headline with its accent word written in (`.sd-hero__ink`, `WRITE` from `src/lib/motion.js`), the sentence and the actions rise 90ms apart, then the frame lifts in, the chips follow it one by one, and **the photograph settles** - one 9s push from 1.05 to 1 on the individual `scale` property (`sd-settle`, keyed on `.sd-frame[data-in]`), the hospital hero's one slow move, never repeated. It used to rise on mount regardless, which spent the head under the curtain on a link opened straight to a service. Measured at 390x664: head from 0.4s, frame 0.7-1.4s, accent written 1.0-1.6s, chips by 1.8s; on a first visit nothing moves until the curtain has gone. The sentence is `text-wrap: balance` - `you.` sat alone on its own line on a 390px phone.

**On a phone held sideways (`max-width: 1179px` and `max-height: 520px`) the headline takes the width and a size the height can afford** (`28ch`, `clamp(2rem, 5vw, 2.6rem)`) and the frame returns to the desktop's `21/9`. At 844x390 and 740x360 a two-line `LASIK & Refractive Surgery` or `Comprehensive Eye Examination` put `Book appointment` 7-18px under the fold, and a 4:3 frame 796px wide was 597px tall. There the two hero actions also share a row rather than stacking full width - on a 568x320 phone the emergency page's stacked booking ended 1px under the fold. Measured: one line and every hero action on the first screen for all eleven at 844x390, 740x360, 667x375 and 568x320.

**Below 640px the hero sheds four things, and the count is the argument.** A phone hero was rendering **twenty-one separate pieces of text** before the reader reached any content. `Included` appeared four times, `Bharuch` twice, and the two glass tags say what the reader already knows - the kind of care is how they got here, and the part of the eye is what the first tab below opens on. Call and WhatsApp go too, because the action bar carrying both rises the moment this hero scrolls away. That leaves **eleven**: a back link, the title, the sentence, one button, one label, four chips and one note - one action, one picture, one list. **If something is added back to this hero, take something out.**

### ServiceExplore - the card

Everything a patient wants to know about one service, in one card they explore rather than four bands they scroll past. **Three tabs: Overview, Why it happens, Signs to notice.** `How we treat it` was a fourth; its `featureBullets` are the hero's glass chips now.

- **Tabs are built from what the service actually has.** A routine eye examination and optical services have no `causes`, so that tab is not rendered rather than rendered empty - they show two tabs where the other nine show three.
- **Overview** is the hospital's paragraphs beside `EyeModel` turned to this service's part, captioned with the part's name and the finder's own sentence for it. The model takes **no choosing handlers** - the part is a fact about this service, not a control - but the reader can still turn it. On a phone the drawing goes first: a patient recognises the part of the eye before they read about it. **The `order: -1` sits on the figure's wrapper (`.sd-split__figure`), which is the grid child** - written on `.sd-area` inside it, it did nothing and the prose led on every phone until the audit caught it. Verified: the figure's top is above the prose's at 390px.
- **The overview caption's eyebrow is `page.detail.areaLabel`** (`Area of the eye`). It lived under `page.detail.hero` while the component read it one level up, so the eyebrow rendered empty on every service until the deployment pass.
- **Why it happens stands in three columns from 901px** (`grid-auto-flow: column`, so any count shares one row), each under a large serif numeral in the accent. As rows they were three one-line sentences in a 1240px card with two thirds of every row empty.
- **Signs to notice** is the interactive one. Each symptom is a button; choosing one draws it on the Snellen chart through `SymptomScene`, under the index's own `How it can look` eyebrow (`finder.sceneLabel`). On a phone the chart leads and the list follows, where a thumb can reach it. **From 901px the chart is the whole chart** (`aspect-ratio: 260 / 392`, all eight rows) rather than the phone's cut of the top three, which read as a thumbnail with its fourth row sliced through the letters. The hint says `Choose a sign`, not `Tap`, because a desktop reader has no finger. The card's foot carries the note that these are descriptions, not a diagnosis.
- Content swaps in the direction the pill travels (framer `custom`), so a switch reads as moving along a row rather than as the page changing underneath. The filled pill is one element that travels (`layoutId`), not a background that blinks off one and on to the next.
- **Below 641px the tabs are one segmented control that fills the row, with short labels** (`tabsShort`: Overview / Causes / Signs; each tab carries both labels and CSS picks one, the cookie sheet's device, and its full name as `aria-label`, because the hidden one is `display: none`). The full three did not fit a phone's card: `Signs to notice` was cut at the card's edge at 390px and choosing it cut `Overview` at the other end, behind a scroller nothing said could scroll. Wider than that the full labels sit in a scroller that never needs to scroll; the chosen tab is centred by writing `scrollLeft` on its own scroller, never with `scrollIntoView`, which would scroll the page as well.
- **It is a real tablist**: the chosen tab is the only one in the tab order, and arrow keys and Home / End walk the tabs (`onTabKey`, focus with `preventScroll`).
- **The card grows and shrinks to the tab rather than snapping to it**, so the page never jumps under the reader's thumb. Two traps, both of which cost a debugging pass:
  - The height is written **straight to the DOM**, not held in state: a state setter here trips `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect`, and the value is a measurement, not something the render depends on.
  - It is a **callback ref, not an effect keyed on the active tab.** `AnimatePresence mode="wait"` holds the outgoing panel until its exit finishes, so an effect that fires when the tab changes measures the panel that is _leaving_ - and then its `ResizeObserver` reports zero the moment that panel detaches, collapsing the card to 0px. Measured: with the effect version the frame read 0 on every tab after the first; with the callback ref it tracks each panel exactly (overview 632, causes 251, signs 515, treatment 327 at 390px).
- **Nothing here navigates.** Choosing a sign changes the chart beside it and nothing else; the URL never changes.

### ServiceActionBar

Most people open a hospital website on a phone, often in a waiting room or on the way in, and the thing they want is to book or to call - not to scroll back to the top to find the button.

- It is **`position: sticky; bottom: 0` inside `<main>`, never `position: fixed`.** Sticky means it releases at the end of the page instead of sitting on top of the footer, so it needs no spacer, fights nothing for z-index, and hands the screen back exactly where the footer's own appointment CTA takes over. A fixed bar would also cover the drawer's thumb-zone actions and the cookie sheet. Verified: pinned to the bottom of the viewport mid-page, released above the footer at the end.
- **Phones and small tablets only** (<= 900px). On a desktop the hero's actions are never far, and a bar across the bottom of a wide screen is clutter.
- Below 560px the two quiet actions drop to glyphs and `Book appointment` keeps its words: three full labels cannot share a 358px row at a 48px target.
- On Emergency Eye Care the call action is the helpline and turns red.

### ServiceActions, and the branch the page offers

- The phone number and the WhatsApp thread belong to **the hospital the reader chose in the header** (`getStoredBranch()`), and the branch name is printed under the label, because a number should never be offered without saying whose it is.
- **The page follows the header while the reader is still on it.** `storeBranch()` dispatches `aakash:branch-change` and the page listens, because `localStorage` fires no event in the tab that wrote it - a page that only read the key on mount would go on offering Visnagar's number after the header had moved to Bharuch.
- `Book appointment` carries `?service=<id>&branch=<slug>`, which `AppointmentPage` reads to prefill the form.
- Emergency Eye Care inverts the hero: the helpline is the filled action and booking is the outline one, because an appointment is the wrong answer to the one service that cannot wait for one.

### ServiceVisit, ServiceFaq, ServiceRelated

- **`ServiceVisit` is a track the reader scrubs, not a list they scroll.** Six numbered dots on a progress rail carry the order at a glance; the panel under them answers what actually happens for whichever one is pressed. The travelled part of the rail fills in behind the reader (`--sd-progress` on the track, `scaleX` on its `::after` - an `<ol>` takes only `<li>` children, so the fill is no longer a span inside it), and passed dots keep a navy outline, so the control is the sequence and the position at once.
  - **Each stop is a button holding the dot and its name** (`.sd-step__stop` > `.sd-step__dot` + `.sd-step__label`). **From 901px the track is a grid of `--sd-steps` equal columns with the name under each dot**, so it reads as a route with named stops rather than six numbers, the rail runs between the first and last columns' centres (`calc(50% / var(--sd-steps))`), and the panel's heading is kept for a screen reader only while the sentence is set at display size - printed on the track and again forty pixels below, the name read as a stutter. Below 901px the names are hidden and the stops are the 32px dots they always were.
  - **The route assembles the first time a third of it is on screen** (`data-in` from an `IntersectionObserver` at 0.35, once): the grey track draws across, then the six dots land along it in order on a small overshoot and each name rises a beat behind its dot - the index pathway's device, because the order is the content. Individual `scale` / `translate`, so it composes with the chosen dot's transform. Each dot's landing delay is per-property (`--sd-land`), so later colour changes are never delayed, and under reduced motion the transitions are removed outright - the global rule zeroes durations but not delays.
  - The lede ends on a hint picked by the pointer, not the width: `Choose a step...` with a mouse, `Swipe or tap a step...` under `(hover: none)` (`visit.hint` / `visit.hintTouch`).
  - **Its foot names every hospital that offers the service, each the way into that hospital's page** (`.sd-visit__places`: the hospitals with a page whose `getBranchServices()` includes this service, in the site's order, as `.e-link`s under the availability note's words). It replaced one link labelled with the reader's hospital that went to the index - the service pages linked to no hospital page at all, the strongest local link a treatment page can carry. The rows are 20px apart because each link's 10px halo reaches up and down; the list sits inside `.sd-visit__copy` so it aligns with the words whether they sit beside the pin or under it. Measured: no overflow and every link hit-testing to itself over its halo at 320, 390, 768 and 1440px, and real touch taps opening the right hospital.
  - **The build before it named the six steps and explained none of them**: six rows of a numbered circle and one word, each two thirds empty, 771px of phone screen to say almost nothing. It read as a skeleton. The stepper is 660px at 390px _and_ has content in it.
  - **It shows the index pathway's step descriptions**, which that section owns. The rule it bends was written when this page had no room for them; one step at a time costs about 84px, where six stacked paragraphs on eleven service pages is what the rule exists to prevent. `visit.pathwayLabel` went with the change - a link to a page that now says the same thing is not a way out.
  - **No numeral in the panel.** The dot the reader just pressed is the number; printing it again one line below said the same thing twice.
  - **The steps are a native `scroll-snap` rail, so a phone reader swipes them.** Same mechanism as the doctor rail, and for the same reasons: the browser gives momentum and snapping for free, and - the part that matters most here - **the direction lock**. Verified with real touch events (`Input.dispatchTouchEvent`, not a synthetic scroll): a horizontal drag moves one step and leaves the page where it was; a vertical drag over the same rail scrolls the page 286px and does not change the step. **Do not replace it with a drag handler** - that would mean reimplementing the lock, badly.
  - **Scroll position is the single source of truth.** The dots do not set the step; they scroll the rail and the rail reports back, which is what keeps the two in sync no matter which one the reader used. The panel is exactly one rail wide, so the index is `scrollLeft / clientWidth` with no stride arithmetic and no gap to keep in sync.
  - **The fill carries no transition of its own.** The scroll handler writes `--sd-progress` every frame, so the rail fills under a dragging thumb instead of lagging a fixed 420ms behind it; a dot press animates because the smooth scroll it starts does. Measured settling exactly at 0.000 and 1.000 at the two ends - sample after the scroll has finished, not during it, or a long desktop jump reads as a wrong value.
  - **All six panels are in the row, so its height is the tallest of them** and the card never resizes as the reader moves through. That replaced a measured `min-height` floor which had to be re-measured whenever a description changed.
  - `aria-current="step"` marks the open one and each dot is labelled `Step 3: Consultation`, so a reader tabbing through hears the whole sequence. Not a tablist: these are steps, not tabs, and a roving tabstop would hide five of the six names.
  - **Six 44px tap targets need 264px between them and a 320px screen has 288.** The dot stays 32px and its stop carries a 6px `::after` ring (`.sd-step__stop::after`), and **the card's padding is what gives** - at 18px the rings overlapped by 3px and a thumb near a boundary opened the wrong step; 12px below 360px clears it. Measured: 48px targets and a 12px gap at 320px, no overlap at any width. The dots never shrink.
  - Without JavaScript the rail is still a plain horizontal scroller holding all six steps, so the content is never trapped behind a control that did not load.
- **`ServiceFaq` carries six or seven questions per service**, as native `<details>`: they open without JavaScript, the browser gives them keyboard behaviour and expanded state for free, and ctrl-F finds answers that are still closed. Several can be open at once, because a reader comparing two answers should not have to keep re-opening the first.
  - **The head is its own column beside the list from 901px**, and sticky at `top: calc(var(--hd-height) + 44px)` so it stays beside whichever question the reader is on and parks below the header rather than sliding under it on a scroll up. Stacked below 901px, where it stops sticking.
  - **The open row lifts off the list as a white card** on the paper ground, its number turns accent and the `+` rotates into an `x`. Hairlines belong to the row _below_ (`.sd-faq__item + .sd-faq__item::before`), never around each row, so an open row never carries a rule against its own rounded corner.
  - **`Ask us directly` sits after the last answer, not in the head.** A reader who still has a question has it there - on a phone the head is a screen and a half above by then.
  - The glide is `::details-content` with `interpolate-size: allow-keywords`, scoped to the list because the property inherits. Verified opening mid-flight at 79 -> 150 -> 183px; a browser without support snaps, exactly as before.
- **The visit, the questions and the way on share one paper, so a section that follows another closes its top padding** (`.sd-sec + .sd-sec`). Their two paddings met as one empty band - 152px between the last question and `Other services` on a laptop. The emergency page's rust band is not an `.sd-sec`, so the questions after it keep their full padding.
- Related services are **rows, not cards** - the index already reads as a ledger, and a row says the same thing in a third of the height. They use the index's own 240px thumbnails, so this ships no new bytes. `getRelatedServices()` takes the same kind of care first and then widens, which is what keeps it honest for Emergency Eye Care, the only service in its category.

### Emergency Eye Care

Same shape, two changes: the hero leads with the helpline, and `ServiceEmergency` - the index's rust band, which is pending a doctor's sign-off - replaces the visit band, because six steps that all begin with booking are the wrong answer there. It takes a `showMore` prop so its closing link does not point at the page the reader is already on, and **a `label` / `title` pair** (`page.detail.urgent`: `When to call` / `Signs that cannot wait.`): the band's own pair is `Emergency eye care` over `Some eye conditions require urgent evaluation.`, which on this page repeated the hero's title and its opening sentence word for word.

### The motion, and the rule it follows

**The hero arrives, the page rises into place as it is scrolled, and everything else answers the reader.** Motion here is orientation and feedback, never decoration, and every piece of it renders finished under `prefers-reduced-motion` (verified: hero at opacity 1 with no transform and the photograph at its final size, the eye lit on the first frame, the route drawn, related rows and panel rows all at 1, 0 running animations).

- **The hero arrives** in reading order after the curtain, the accent writes itself in and the photograph settles (see ServiceHero).
- **The rest rises as the reader scrolls** - the services index's model, in the block before the reduced-motion rules at the end of `service-detail.css`: every value a function of scroll position (`animation-timeline: view()`), on the compositor, nothing pinned. The heads cascade (`.sd-head > *`, `.sd-faq__head > *`), the explore card and the visit card rise over the first half of their entry, and the availability note, each question and the closing ask come up one by one. It replaced `Reveal`, which faded from 0.96 and moved 10px - so little that the lower half of the page read as still. Firefox gets the page static, as on the index. Measured at 390x664, 768x1024 and 1440x900: every element in the upper 62% of the screen at opacity 1 with no translate at every scroll position. Nothing animated here may sit under an `overflow: hidden` ancestor.
- **The visit's route assembles** once it is a third on screen (see ServiceVisit).
- **The action bar rises when the hero's own actions leave the screen** and drops back when they return, so a reader never sees the same button twice. It watches the element (`IntersectionObserver` on the hero's action row, passed down as a ref - React 19 takes `ref` as an ordinary prop) rather than a scroll offset, because the hero is a different height on every service and at every width. Off-screen it is `pointer-events: none; visibility: hidden`, so it can never take a tap or a tab stop while it is out of view.
- **The visit is scrubbed, not stepped through.** The panels swipe with the finger and the rail's travelled portion fills behind them frame by frame; under reduced motion a dot press jumps the rail to the exact step and the active dot loses its scale pop, but the step still changes.
- **The card's contents assemble.** The panel slides in the direction the pill travelled and its rows arrive behind it on a 60ms stagger. Measured mid-flight: the first row at 0.17 while the rest are still at 0, all settled at 1. A tab that assembles reads as a different view of the same card; a tab that appears whole reads as the page having been replaced.
- **The eye turns to the part a beat after it lands** (`LIGHT_UP_MS`, 420ms). `EyeModel` mounts with no active part - the cutaway at rest - and `AreaFigure` switches it on, so the reader watches the eye turn to the answer instead of being handed it.
